When his wife pointed out that he was withdrawing from friends and skipping church and hunting trips, and he noticed his performance reviews mentioning increased irritability and disengagement, he agreed to see a counselor through his health plan to get help adjusting to all the recent changes.\n- Chief Complaint: “With all the changes at work and at home, I just can’t seem to get my feet back under me or handle things the way I used to.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S081 (Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response): Ever since they switched me to the city shifts and the house went quiet with the kids gone, I feel like I’ve been overreacting to everything—snapping at my wife over little schedule changes, ducking calls from my fishing buddies, dragging my feet at work, and just hiding out in the garage because I don’t know how to deal with how upside down everything suddenly feels.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- within_3_months_of_stressor: Within a few weeks of being moved to the city rotation and the house going quiet after our youngest left for college, I started pulling back from friends, arguing more with my wife, and feeling like I just couldn’t get a handle on things.\n- within_6_months_of_termination: I keep telling myself that if they ever switched me back to my old 24-on/48-off rural schedule or things at home felt more like they used to, I’d Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in today. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: With all the changes at work and at home, I just can’t seem to get my feet back under me or handle things the way I used to. It feels like everything shifted at once, and I'm just struggling to keep up.\n\nDoctor: Did these difficulties start after a specific stressful event or major life change, and have they been going on for more than six months?\n\nPatient: Yes, it all started about four and a half months ago when my work switched me to unpredictable city shifts right as our youngest left for college. These struggles kicked in pretty much right after those changes, so it's been going on for about four months now, which is less than six.\n\nDoctor: Since these changes started, have you been feeling more depressed or anxious than usual, or having trouble functioning at work or at home because of it?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'm definitely having trouble functioning both at home and at work. Even though I'm feeling really overwhelmed and stressed by all these sudden changes, I don't have those kinds of thoughts about hurting myself or ending my life.\n\nDoctor: Since these changes started, have you been feeling more sad, hopeless, or worried than usual most days?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say it's deep sadness or hopelessness, but I definitely feel completely unmoored and overwhelmed. Mostly, I'm just highly irritable and on edge, snapping at my wife and struggling to handle these new changes.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling mainly anxious or worried, or mainly sad and hopeless, most days since these changes started?\n\nPatient: It’s not really intense anxiety or deep hopelessness, but rather a feeling of being completely unmoored, overwhelmed, and irritable. I’ve been snapping at my wife, withdrawing from my friends, and just hiding out in the garage because everything feels so upside down. I just feel unable to cope with how much things have changed.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms started to improve at all as you've adjusted to the changes, or have they been getting worse or staying the same?\n\nPatient: They've pretty much been staying the same and remain a constant struggle. It's been about four months of feeling this way, and I haven't really noticed any improvement or felt like I've gotten my feet back under me yet.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms been tied to one or more specific stressful life changes, and do they feel out of proportion to what you would normally expect from those changes?\n\nPatient: Yes, they are definitely tied to my work switching me to those unpredictable city shifts and my youngest leaving for college. It absolutely feels out of proportion, because I'm snapping at my wife over minor things and withdrawing from my friends, which is completely unlike how I normally handle stress.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar reaction to stress before, or is this the first time you’ve felt this overwhelmed and unable to cope after a life change?\n\nPatient: This is the first time I've ever felt this overwhelmed and unable to cope. As an EMT, I'm very used to handling high-stress emergencies on the job, but these personal and routine changes have hit me in a completely new and challenging way.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms started to ease as you’ve adjusted to the changes, or do they still feel just as strong even now?\n\nPatient: They still feel just as strong, and I really haven't adjusted to any of this yet after four months. Honestly, I feel like this rough patch would only start to ease up if my work schedule or home life went back to the way things used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms started within three months of the stressful changes, or did they begin later than that?"

Because the unpredictable work schedule and the empty house are still my daily reality, I just haven't been able to find my footing yet. I really think things would only start to ease up if my circumstances ever changed back to how they used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have these stress-related symptoms been causing problems in your daily functioning, such as at work, at home, or in your relationships?"

Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.
